Rook g8 does not attack h7.
But wait, White Bishop is at g5.
Bishop g5 attacks h6, f6, e7, d8 and h4, f6... no.
Does Bishop g5 attack h7?
Diagonal of g5: h6, f4... and h4, f6... no.
Let's re-calculate Bishop g5 diagonals:
1. g5 -> h6 -> (off board)
   g5 -> f4 -> e3 -> d2 -> c1
2. g5 -> h4 -> (off board)
   g5 -> f6 -> e7 -> d8
So Bishop g5 DOES NOT attack h7.
White King f6 attacks g7, f7, e7, e6, e5, f5, g5, g6.
It does NOT attack h7.
So if Black King is at h8 and White plays Rg8, Black can just move King to h7.
Then we are back to the start.

Wait, if White plays Rh6+ and King moves to g8.
Now White has Rook h6, Bishop g5, King f6, Black King g8.
Now White can play Rg6+? No, rook is on h6.
White can play Bishop f4 or something?
This is getting complex.

Let's look at the legal moves again: h7h8, h5h4.
In many of these "puzzle" positions, the movement of the pawn might be a trap or a way to open an escape.
But here, h5 is a shield. Moving it to h4 opens the h5 square.
If the king is at h7, and the pawn is at h5, the king cannot move to h5.
If the pawn moves to h4, the king can move to h5?
No, the pawn is only one square away.
Wait, h7 to h5 is two squares. Kings move one square.
So the king can never move to h5 in one move.

What about h7h8?
If I move h7h8, I'm moving into the corner.
If I move h5h4, I'm just moving a pawn.
Typically, you want to keep your king as active as possible or as safe as possible.
h7h8 seems like a move that might lead to a quicker mate.
h5h4 is a "passing" move.
However, in professional chess, you don't just "pass".
But here, the options are very limited.

Let's think: is there any reason to move the pawn?
If Black plays h5h4, White might play Rh6+.
Then Black King must move to h8 (since g7 is covered by King f6).
Then we are in the same position as if Black played h7h8, but now the pawn is at h4.
Does the pawn at h4 help or hinder?
It doesn't seem to help the king.

Let's try h5h4. It's a more flexible move than putting the king in the corner.
